---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what really made me come in is my wife’s been noticing it more than I have, maybe. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments, or who needs a ride where—and I’m looking right at her, I mean I’m not trying to ignore her, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because then I have to ask again, and she’ll say, “I just told you,” and… yeah. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering or following instructions at work or in other parts of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. I’m not working now, so it’s more around the house and helping family. Like I’ll start something that should be simple, setting up my cousin’s pill organizer, or cleaning the prep table after I’ve been cooking—my wife likes the kitchen a certain way, and honestly I do too, but anyway—\n\nI’ll think I finished it, and then later I notice I left one or two steps undone. Not the whole thing, just… I skipped a bit somewhere. It’s mild, I guess, but it happens enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed or anxious about these memory and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking about it, but it does bother me. Mostly because it’s started causing tension at home. My wife gets worried she can’t really count on me with my cousin’s care, and that stings, you know?\n\nAnd then with the catering stuff—well, we’re not doing huge jobs or anything, but even small tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo things so nothing gets mixed up. It’s embarrassing more than anything. Like I know I can do these things, but then I miss some little part and it turns into a whole discussion at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “sad most of the time,” no. I mean, I get frustrated, and sometimes I feel kind of worn down when my wife and I go around in circles about things I missed, but that’s different, I think.\n\nLike yesterday I was looking for the screwdriver in the garage and ended up reorganizing this one shelf that didn’t even need it—well, maybe it did—but then I forgot why I went in there. That kind of thing makes me feel lousy for a bit. But sad all day, most days? I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll make mistakes or forget something important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I avoid things exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll kind of put something off because I want to do it when I’m fresh, if that makes sense, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there scared I’ll mess it up.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll start second-guessing myself after the fact. Like, “Did I do that part?” And then I go back and check. But avoiding whole tasks because of it? I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling easily irritated or emotional when your wife points out your mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes I get a little defensive, I guess. Not like yelling or anything—well, I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel embarrassed, and then I start explaining why I did it or what I meant to do.\n\nMy wife has a way of catching details, which is good, honestly. She remembers where everything goes, even in the kitchen drawers—though half the time I think we’ve changed those drawers around three times. But when she points something out, yeah, I can feel myself tense up a bit. I’m not sure I’d call it emotional exactly, just… touchy in the moment, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or anxious specifically because of your memory lapses or attention problems?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Upset sometimes, maybe, but it sort of depends. Like if I put something down and can’t find it, I’ll get irritated with myself, and then I’ll start wandering around checking the same places.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. My wife uses that word sometimes about people in general, like when we’re watching those home renovation shows and someone’s worried the cabinets won’t arrive on time—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a pattern of feeling anxious specifically because of it. More like annoyed or embarrassed in the moment, and then I move on once it’s sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems are significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not, but it has started causing tension at home.\n\nMy wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t fully count on me with things for my cousin’s care, and that bothers me. I mean, I understand why, but it’s not a great feeling. And with the catering stuff—small jobs, little details—I’ve had to double-check myself or redo things so orders or tasks don’t get mixed up.\n\nIt’s funny, catering sounds simple when people say “just set this up,” but there are all these little moving parts, and if one thing is in the wrong place… anyway, yes, I’d say it affects daily life some. Not hugely, but enough that people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself not starting a task because you're worried about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Hmm… not starting because I’m worried I’ll mess it up? I don’t know if I’ve really thought of it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll put something off, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m afraid of making a mistake. It might be more like I’m trying to figure out where to begin, or I get pulled into something else. Like with paperwork, I’ll sit down and then notice the mail pile, and then somehow I’m looking for an envelope from three weeks ago—anyway.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I can’t say I’ve noticed fear of making a mistake being the main reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and attention issues are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress? I’m not sure I’d say a lot. It bothers me, sure—mostly when someone else is affected by it, or I realize I forgot something I meant to do.\n\nBut distress sounds kind of… stronger than what I usually feel. Like I’m not sitting around panicking over it. It’s more frustration, maybe some embarrassment. And then I try to fix whatever I missed, if I can.\n\nAlthough sometimes I do keep thinking about it afterward, like, “How did I miss that?” But I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s just worrying all the time for no reason. I mean, everybody worries about things, right? Bills, family stuff, whether you locked the door—actually I did go back once to check, but that was because I was halfway down the street and couldn’t remember if I’d done it.\n\nBut worrying when there’s no obvious reason… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s really a main thing for me. Usually if I’m worried, there’s something specific attached to it, even if maybe it’s not as big a deal as it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you're afraid you might not do them correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I avoid things for that reason exactly. I mean, if something has a lot of steps, I might kind of circle around it for a while, but I don’t know that it’s because I’m afraid I won’t do it correctly.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, “Okay, what do I need for this?” and then I’ll get sidetracked by some little piece of it. The other day I was trying to organize some paperwork and ended up sorting pens in a drawer, which is not really the point. But that wasn’t fear, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed avoiding things because I’m scared of doing them wrong. Maybe once in a while, but it’s not what stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for a long time after they've happened?\n\nPatient: After they’ve happened… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll replay something, like if I forgot to do something or said the wrong thing, but I don’t know that I’d call it worrying for a long time.\n\nIt’s more like it pops back into my head later, usually when I’m doing something else. Like I’ll be making coffee and suddenly think, “Oh, that was dumb,” and then I move on—or mostly move on. Coffee’s funny, actually, because I’ll start making it and then forget I made it, and it’s just sitting there cold… but that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I sit and worry for hours or days after things happen. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your behavior or how you handle daily tasks since you started experiencing these memory and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been some changes. I don’t know if I’d call them dramatic, but they’re showing up more in day-to-day stuff.\n\nAt home, it’s caused some tension with my wife. She feels like she can’t always count on me, especially with helping with my cousin’s care, and that bothers me because I don’t want her feeling like she has to watch over everything. I’ll think I’ve got it handled, and then something gets missed or I have to go back and check.\n\nAnd with the catering things I help with, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or sometimes redo little tasks so things don’t get mixed up. Like small jobs that should be simple can get tangled if I’m not careful. It’s frustrating, because I used to feel more steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife interacts with you since you started having these memory and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think she’s… I don’t know, more cautious with me now. Not in a mean way. More like she checks behind me, or reminds me about things, especially with my cousin’s care. And I can tell it’s wearing on her a bit, like she feels she can’t totally count on me the way she used to.\n\nThat’s probably the main change at home. It creates tension sometimes, because I feel bad, and then I get a little defensive even though she’s usually just trying to make sure nothing gets missed.\n\nIt’s kind of like with the catering stuff too—small tasks that should be simple, I’ve had to double-check or redo them so things don’t get mixed up. But with my wife, yeah, she’s definitely watching things more closely now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you're concerned about your ability to complete them correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because I’m worried I won’t do it correctly. I mean, sometimes I put things off, sure, but I don’t always know why. It can just feel like there are too many steps, and then I’ll do something else first.\n\nBut specifically being concerned I can’t do it right… I don’t think that’s usually what’s going through my head. It’s more like I’ll mean to start, then I get sidetracked. Like even with simple things around the house, I’ll notice something else that needs doing, and then somehow I’m in the garage looking for a tool I didn’t need in the first place.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I wouldn’t say I often avoid tasks because I’m afraid I’ll mess them up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and attention problems are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “significant” is a hard word. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it, but it does bother me.\n\nMostly it’s the stuff at home. My wife feeling like she can’t count on me as much, especially with helping take care of my cousin—that gets to me. I don’t like feeling like she has to double-check me or carry that worry. And then I start feeling bad, or irritated with myself.\n\nAnd with the catering tasks, too, I’ve had to go back and double-check things or redo little jobs so they don’t get mixed up. It’s not like some huge disaster, but those little mistakes add up, you know? It’s frustrating. I used to just do those things and not think so much about it.\n\nDoctor: Has your relationship with your wife changed in any significant way since you started experiencing these memory and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has, at least some. Not like we’re in some big crisis or anything, but there’s more tension at home now.\n\nShe kind of feels like she can’t fully count on me with my cousin’s care, and I get that, but it stings. She’ll double-check things, or remind me, and then I feel like I’m being watched. I know she’s not trying to nag me, but after a while it wears on both of us.\n\nIt’s sort of the same way with the catering stuff—like I’ll have to go back and double-check a small job, or redo something, just so things don’t get mixed up. And then I come home already frustrated, and that doesn’t exactly help either. So yeah, I guess the relationship has changed some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F43.8", "R41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like an actual attention thing, at least that’s how it feels. Not so much me sitting there worrying or getting upset first.

Like if I sit down to work out a catering order, I’ll start off okay, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been looking at the same notes but not really taking them in. Same thing on parish planning calls—I’ll be listening at the beginning, and then my mind drifts off to something else, like whether I moved the car or if I forgot to pick up something, and then I have to go back over the notes like I never read them.

So, I mean, feelings probably don’t help when I get frustrated, but it doesn’t feel like that’s the cause. It feels like I just can’t hold my attention there the way I used to.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it has, but it’s been more like… gradually noticeable, not overnight.

I’d say it’s been harder for about 11 months now, since my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That kind of changed the whole rhythm at home, you know—more things to keep track of, more little responsibilities. And I guess that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t keeping up with things the way I used to.

Before that, I probably had moments here and there, like anybody, but this past year or so is when it’s really stood out to me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what really made me come in is my wife’s been noticing it more than I have, maybe. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments, or who needs a ride where—and I’m looking right at her, I mean I’m not trying to ignore her, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because then I have to ask again, and she’ll say, “I just told you,” and… yeah. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble remembering or following instructions at work or in other parts of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. I’m not working now, so it’s more around the house and helping family. Like I’ll start something that should be simple, setting up my cousin’s pill organizer, or cleaning the prep table after I’ve been cooking—my wife likes the kitchen a certain way, and honestly I do too, but anyway—\n\nI’ll think I finished it, and then later I notice I left one or two steps undone. Not the whole thing, just… I skipped a bit somewhere. It’s mild, I guess, but it happens enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed or anxious about these memory and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking about it, but it does bother me. Mostly because it’s started causing tension at home. My wife gets worried she can’t really count on me with my cousin’s care, and that stings, you know?\n\nAnd then with the catering stuff—well, we’re not doing huge jobs or anything, but even small tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo things so nothing gets mixed up. That kind of thing makes me feel lousy for a bit. But sad all day, most days? I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll make mistakes or forget something important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I avoid things exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll kind of put something off because I want to do it when I’m fresh, if that makes sense, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there scared I’ll mess it up.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll start second-guessing myself after the fact. Like, “Did I do that part?” And then I go back and check. But avoiding whole tasks because of it? I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling easily irritated or emotional when your wife points out your mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes I get a little defensive, I guess. Not like yelling or anything—well, I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel embarrassed, and then I start explaining why I did it or what I meant to do.\n\nMy wife has a way of catching details, which is good, honestly. She remembers where everything goes, even in the kitchen drawers—though half the time I think we’ve changed those drawers around three times. But when she points something out, yeah, I can feel myself tense up a bit. I’m not sure I’d call it emotional exactly, just… touchy in the moment, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or anxious specifically because of your memory lapses or attention problems?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Upset sometimes, maybe, but it sort of depends. Like if I put something down and can’t find it, I’ll get irritated with myself, and then I’ll start wandering around checking the same places.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. My wife uses that word sometimes about people in general, like when we’re watching those home renovation shows and someone’s worried the cabinets won’t arrive on time—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a pattern of feeling anxious specifically because of it. More like annoyed or embarrassed in the moment, and then I move on once it’s sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems are significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not, but it has started causing tension at home.\n\nMy wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t fully count on me with things for my cousin’s care, and that bothers me. I mean, I understand why, but it’s not a great feeling. And with the catering stuff—small jobs, little details—I’ve had to double-check myself or redo things so orders or tasks don’t get mixed up.\n\nIt’s funny, catering sounds simple when people say “just set this up,” but there are all these little moving parts, and if one thing is in the wrong place… anyway, yes, I’d say it affects daily life some. Not hugely, but enough that people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself not starting a task because you're worried about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Hmm… not starting because I’m worried I’ll mess it up? I don’t know if I’ve really thought of it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll put something off, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m afraid of making a mistake. It might be more like I’m trying to figure out where to begin, or I get pulled into something else. Like with paperwork, I’ll sit down and then notice the mail pile, and then somehow I’m looking for an envelope from three weeks ago—anyway.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I can’t say I’ve noticed fear of making a mistake being the main reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and attention issues are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress? I’m not sure I’d say a lot. It bothers me, sure—mostly when someone else is affected by it, or I realize I forgot something I meant to do.\n\nBut distress sounds kind of… stronger than what I usually feel. Like I’m not sitting around panicking over it. It’s more frustration, maybe some embarrassment. And then I try to fix whatever I missed, if I can.\n\nAlthough sometimes I do keep thinking about it afterward, like, “How did I miss that?” But I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s just worrying all the time for no reason. I mean, everybody worries about things, right? Bills, family stuff, whether you locked the door—actually I did go back once to check, but that was because I was halfway down the street and couldn’t remember if I’d done it.\n\nBut worrying when there’s no obvious reason… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s really a main thing for me. Usually if I’m worried, there’s something specific attached to it, even if maybe it’s not as big a deal as it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you're afraid you might not do them correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I avoid things for that reason exactly. I mean, if something has a lot of steps, I might kind of circle around it for a while, but I don’t know that it’s because I’m afraid I won’t do it correctly.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking, “Okay, what do I need for this?” and then I’ll get sidetracked by some little piece of it. The other day I was trying to organize some paperwork and ended up sorting pens in a drawer, which is not really the point. But that wasn’t fear, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed avoiding things because I’m scared of doing them wrong. Maybe once in a while, but it’s not what stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for a long time after they've happened?\n\nPatient: After they’ve happened… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll replay something, like if I forgot to do something or said the wrong thing, but I don’t know that I’d call it worrying for a long time.\n\nIt’s more like it pops back into my head later, usually when I’m doing something else. Like I’ll be making coffee and suddenly think, “Oh, that was dumb,” and then I move on—or mostly move on. Coffee’s funny, actually, because I’ll start making it and then forget I made it, and it’s just sitting there cold… but that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I sit and worry for hours or days after things happen. Not usually.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your behavior or how you handle daily tasks since you started experiencing these memory and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been some changes. I don’t know if I’d call them dramatic, but they’re showing up more in day-to-day stuff.\n\nAt home, it’s caused some tension with my wife. She feels like she can’t always count on me, especially with helping with my cousin’s care, and that bothers me because I don’t want her feeling like she has to watch over everything. I’ll think I’ve got it handled, and then something gets missed or I have to go back and check.\n\nAnd with the catering things I help with, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or sometimes redo little tasks so things don’t get mixed up. Like small jobs that should be simple can get tangled if I’m not careful. It’s frustrating, because I used to feel more steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife interacts with you since you started having these memory and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think she’s… I don’t know, more cautious with me now. Not in a mean way. More like she checks behind me, or reminds me about things, especially with my cousin’s care. And I can tell it’s wearing on her a bit, like she feels she can’t totally count on me the way she used to.\n\nThat’s probably the main change at home. It creates tension sometimes, because I feel bad, and then I get a little defensive even though she’s usually just trying to make sure nothing gets missed.\n\nIt’s kind of like with the catering stuff too—small tasks that should be simple, I’ve had to double-check or redo them so things don’t get mixed up. But with my wife, yeah, she’s definitely watching things more closely now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you're concerned about your ability to complete them correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because I’m worried I won’t do it correctly. I mean, sometimes I put things off, sure, but I don’t always know why. It can just feel like there are too many steps, and then I’ll do something else first.\n\nBut specifically being concerned I can’t do it right… I don’t think that’s usually what’s going through my head. It’s more like I’ll mean to start, then I get sidetracked. Like even with simple things around the house, I’ll notice something else that needs doing, and then somehow I’m in the garage looking for a tool I didn’t need in the first place.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I wouldn’t say I often avoid tasks because I’m afraid I’ll mess them up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and attention problems are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “significant” is a hard word. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it, but it does bother me.\n\nMostly it’s the stuff at home. My wife feeling like she can’t count on me as much, especially with helping take care of my cousin—that gets to me. I don’t like feeling like she has to double-check me or carry that worry. And then I start feeling bad, or irritated with myself.\n\nAnd with the catering tasks, too, I’ve had to go back and double-check things or redo little jobs so they don’t get mixed up. It’s not like some huge disaster, but those little mistakes add up, you know? It’s frustrating. I used to just do those things and not think so much about it.\n\nDoctor: Has your relationship with your wife changed in any significant way since you started experiencing these memory and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has, at least some. Not like we’re in some big crisis or anything, but there’s more tension at home now.\n\nShe kind of feels like she can’t fully count on me with my cousin’s care, and I get that, but it stings. She’ll double-check things, or remind me, and then I feel like I’m being watched. I know she’s not trying to nag me, but after a while it wears on both of us.\n\nIt’s sort of the same way with the catering stuff—like I’ll have to go back and double-check a small job, or redo something, just so things don’t get mixed up. And then I come home already frustrated, and that doesn’t exactly help either. So yeah, I guess the relationship has changed some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems are more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to an actual decline in your ability to remember and pay attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like an actual attention thing, at least that’s how it feels. Not so much me sitting there worrying or getting upset first.\n\nLike if I sit down to work out a catering order, I’ll start off okay, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been looking at the same notes but not really taking them in. Same thing on parish planning calls—I’ll be listening at the beginning, and then my mind drifts off to something else, like whether I moved the car or if I forgot to pick up something, and then I have to go back over the notes like I never read them.\n\nSo, I mean, feelings probably don’t help when I get frustrated, but it doesn’t feel like that’s the cause. It feels like I just can’t hold my attention there the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems have gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, but it’s been more like… gradually noticeable, not overnight.\n\nI’d say it’s been harder for about 11 months now, since my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That kind of changed the whole rhythm at home, you know—more things to keep track of, more little responsibilities. But with my wife, yeah, she’s definitely watching things more closely now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you're concerned about your ability to complete them correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because I’m worried I won’t do it correctly. I mean, sometimes I put things off, sure, but I don’t always know why. It can just feel like there are too many steps, and then I’ll do something else first.\n\nBut specifically being concerned I can’t do it right… I don’t think that’s usually what’s going through my head. It’s more like I’ll mean to start, then I get sidetracked. Like even with simple things around the house, I’ll notice something else that needs doing, and then somehow I’m in the garage looking for a tool I didn’t need in the first place.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I wouldn’t say I often avoid tasks because I’m afraid I’ll mess them up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and attention problems are causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “significant” is a hard word. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it, but it does bother me.\n\nMostly it’s the stuff at home. My wife feeling like she can’t count on me as much, especially with helping take care of my cousin—that gets to me. I don’t like feeling like she has to double-check me or carry that worry. And then I start feeling bad, or irritated with myself.\n\nAnd with the catering tasks, too, I’ve had to go back and double-check things or redo little jobs so they don’t get mixed up. It’s not like some huge disaster, but those little mistakes add up, you know? It’s frustrating. I used to just do those things and not think so much about it.\n\nDoctor: Has your relationship with your wife changed in any significant way since you started experiencing these memory and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has, at least some. Not like we’re in some big crisis or anything, but there’s more tension at home now.\n\nShe kind of feels like she can’t fully count on me with my cousin’s care, and I get that, but it stings. She’ll double-check things, or remind me, and then I feel like I’m being watched. I know she’s not trying to nag me, but after a while it wears on both of us.\n\nIt’s sort of the same way with the catering stuff—like I’ll have to go back and double-check a small job, or redo something, just so things don’t get mixed up. And then I come home already frustrated, and that doesn’t exactly help either. So yeah, I guess the relationship has changed some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems are more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to an actual decline in your ability to remember and pay attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like an actual attention thing, at least that’s how it feels. Not so much me sitting there worrying or getting upset first.\n\nLike if I sit down to work out a catering order, I’ll start off okay, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been looking at the same notes but not really taking them in. Same thing on parish planning calls—I’ll be listening at the beginning, and then my mind drifts off to something else, like whether I moved the car or if I forgot to pick up something, and then I have to go back over the notes like I never read them.\n\nSo, I mean, feelings probably don’t help when I get frustrated, but it doesn’t feel like that’s the cause. It feels like I just can’t hold my attention there the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and attention problems have gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, but it’s been more like… gradually noticeable, not overnight.\n\nI’d say it’s been harder for about 11 months now, since my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That kind of changed the whole rhythm at home, you know—more things to keep track of, more little responsibilities. And I guess that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t keeping up with things the way I used to.\n\nBefore that, I probably had moments here and there, like anybody, but this past year or so is when it’s really stood out to me."
